Runelords Haiku playtest (v1.230)

Half-Infernal Half-Blood Sorcerer, Level 8
Turns 0–150 · 301 passages
Turn 0

The Swallowtail Festival banners are burning.

You smell it first, not the sharp bite of pine or torch-flame, but something richer, older. Ash drifts through Sandpoint's central square like snow in reverse, rising instead of falling. The cathedral's new bell tolls once, wrong, as if someone is ringing it in panic. Around you, townsfolk have stopped mid-celebration. A child's wooden toy lies abandoned in the dirt. The festival bonfire has grown teeth.

Victor Marlow stands at your shoulder, dark eyes already tracking movement at the square's far edge, the glint of crude spears catching firelight. His hand has found the pommel of one short blade without thinking. "Goblins," he says quietly. "More than a few." Beside him, Peet's fingers are already tracing shapes in the air, his necromantic senses reaching outward like roots seeking rot. His dark robes absorb the firelight. The shade-touched hollow-born's breath comes steady, practiced. No fear there. Just the cold catalogue of death-magic preparing its catalogue.

The screaming hasn't started yet. But it will.

I rally Victor and Peet and drive the goblins back from the festival square, protecting the fleeing townsfolk and the cathedral steps.

Turn 1

You move without hesitation. That costs you nothing, the composure is the tool, not the burden. Not yet.

"Victor, left flank. Peet, funnel them toward the cathedral steps, nowhere to run but up." Your words cut through the chaos like a blade through silk. Victor's already moving, blades drawing moonlight as he slides between panicking families and a shrieking goblin pack. Three of them break formation to chase him, he wants that. Peet raises one hand and whispers something ancient and wet. The ground beneath a cluster of goblins ripples. Skeletal arms erupt from the dirt, bone-white and wrong, seizing two of them and slamming them prone.

You step forward. Your dark glass rod hums in your grip, a subsonic frequency only you truly feel, warm as blood. The nearest goblin shrieks something in its own language, a sound like a kettle boiling dry. You don't bother with magic. The rod swings. His ribs cave inward. He doesn't get back up.

The townsfolk are running now, toward the cathedral. Good. A merchant woman pulls three children behind her. An old fisherman grabs a burning brand from the bonfire and waves it at the goblins, more courage than sense, but it works. They flinch.

Victor has already gutted one. His blade moves like it's apologizing to the goblin for how fast it's happening. Two more converge on him, crude swords raised.

How do you press the advantage?

I cut down the goblin raiders threatening Father Zantus at the podium, holding the line until the square is clear.

Turn 2

You move into the gap between chaos and sanctuary. The cathedral steps are a chokepoint, good ground if you hold it.

Father Abstalar Zantus stands frozen at the podium, his ceremonial robes still pristine, his face the colour of old parchment. Three goblins are closing on him, shrieking their little war-songs. They don't see you until you're already there.

The first one dies wondering what happened. Your rod catches him under the jaw, subsonic resonance meeting soft flesh in a way that leaves him twitching on the steps. The second tries to dodge. You don't let him. A side sweep takes his legs out from under him, and he tumbles backward down the stone. The third one actually manages to swing at you, a crude curved blade that catches your arm. Blood opens along your forearm, not deep, but it burns.

You slide past his follow-up without effort. Your rod comes up and crushes his throat. He makes a sound like a wounded animal and doesn't make another.

"Get inside," you tell Zantus. The priest moves, limbs jerking with shock. Behind you, Victor has cornered two goblins against the fountain. Peet's skeletal shackles are still holding three more, pinned and thrashing. The square is clearing, most of the townsfolk have fled into the cathedral or toward the Rusty Dragon.

A horn sounds from the southern edge of town. Long, low, and hungry. More goblins. Many more. Organized.

Whoever called this raid knew what they were doing.

With the raid broken, I find Sheriff Hemlock amid the wreckage and offer our help, asking what he knows about who sent these goblins.

Turn 3

Sheriff Belor Hemlock emerges from the smoke like a man walking through a fever dream. His uniform is scorched. Blood, not his, you think, streaks his left sleeve. He's older than you expected, grey threading through his beard, but his eyes are sharp enough. They find you first, then Victor, then Peet. He does the math quickly.

"You three stopped that?" He doesn't wait for an answer. "Licktoad tribe. Scum from the brush south of town, they've been getting bolder for months, but this..." He gestures at the burning banners, the overturned stalls. "This was coordinated. Disciplined. Licktoads don't do discipline."

He pulls a scorched scrap of parchment from his belt. There's a symbol on it, a seven-pointed star, elegant and wrong. The lines seem to catch the firelight differently than they should. Your breath catches slightly. You know that shape. You've seen it in your sealed archive folio, in pages marked with warnings your predecessor left in blood-notation margins. Thassilonian. Ancient. Dangerous.

"Found it on one of the goblin leaders before he bolted," Hemlock says. He's watching your face. "You recognize it."

Victor steps closer, squinting at the symbol. Peet doesn't move, but his eyes have gone distant, the way they do when he's reading something written in death rather than ink.

The question hangs unasked: what do you tell him?

I examine the goblin corpses and their gear for anything unusual — a banner, a brand, a token pointing to who organized and armed them.

Turn 4

You move methodically through the square. Victor shadows you without being asked, checking each body with the efficiency of someone who's done this before. Peet follows, his gaze lingering on the corpses in a way that makes the shade-touched hollow-born useful for more than just combat, he reads echoes in flesh.

The goblins are poorly armed. Crude scimitars. Leather scraps. Nothing organized. But beneath the randomness, you find patterns. Three of them carry identical iron armbands, a crude casting, but consistent. A symbol worked into the metal: the same seven-pointed star. Greed's mark.

Victor produces a small silk bundle from one goblin's pouch. Inside: a vial of something dark and viscous. "Poison or drug," he murmurs. "Keeps them running hot. Look at their pupils." He's right. Even in death, their eyes are blown wide. Stimulant, not poison. Courage in a bottle.

Peet kneels beside a larger goblin, the leader, by the size and the better quality of his gear. His fingers brush the corpse's temple. For a moment his eyes unfocus, reading the final moments written in bone and meat. When he speaks, his voice is flat. "He was afraid. Not of you. Of whoever sent him. Someone gave orders. He knew the star meant death if he failed."

No banner. No written orders. Whoever organized this knew better than to leave paper trails. But the armbands tell you something: this wasn't improvised desperation. It was orchestrated. Equipped. Someone with resources and knowledge of Thassilonian symbolism wanted these goblins to carry a message.

Hemlock watches you work. He's waiting for you to speak.

What do you tell the sheriff?

I seek out Ameiko at the Rusty Dragon and the townsfolk to learn what the goblins were really after.

Turn 5

The Rusty Dragon is chaos wrapped in wood and candlelight. Townsfolk pack the common room, families huddled in corners, merchants counting their losses, a few of the younger men nursing bruises and ale in equal measure. The air tastes like smoke and fear.

Ameiko Kaijitsu stands behind the bar, moving with the kind of focused efficiency that comes from years of keeping a tavern running through disaster. She's Tian-blooded, dark-haired, sharp-eyed, the sort of woman who notices everything and forgets nothing. When she sees you, she stops.

"You stopped them," she says. Not a question. "When they came screaming through the square, I thought..." She trails off, jaw tightening. "They weren't after coin or goods. They were looking for something specific. Or someone."

She leans forward on her elbows. Several townsfolk have gone quiet, listening.

"They tore through the common room like they were searching. Overturned tables, smashed bottles, didn't steal a damn thing. One of them kept shouting in Goblin, I don't speak it, but the tone..." She shakes her head. "They wanted to find someone. A person. When they couldn't, they just burned."

An old woman near the hearth, Netali Broome, you remember, a farmer's widow, speaks up quietly. "My grandson said they had a leader. Tall for a goblin. Scarred. He kept pointing at people, checking them against something. Like he had names."

The goblins were hunting specific targets. Not random slaughter. A list.

I follow the strongest lead about the raid's true purpose, pressing toward whoever directed the attack.

Turn 6

You sit with Ameiko at a corner table, away from the crowd's ears. Victor positions himself near the door, habit, mostly. Peet settles into shadow like it's a second skin.

"The goblin leader," you say quietly. "Did anyone see which direction he went?"

Ameiko's jaw tightens. "South. Toward the brush and the coast road. But..." She hesitates, and you catch the moment she decides to trust you. "There's more. My father, Lonjiku, he's been acting strange for weeks. Secretive meetings. Money moving through accounts that shouldn't exist. And last month, he brought in a shipment marked 'glasswork supplies' that I know wasn't glass."

She lowers her voice further. "I think he's involved in something. Something bigger than just running the family business. When the goblins attacked, he wasn't in the square. He wasn't at the Glassworks either. I checked after. And now..." She swallows hard. "Now I can't find him."

Victor leans back slightly, processing. Peet's eyes have gone distant again, reading the weight of what Ameiko just admitted, the fear underneath her words.

This isn't a simple tribal raid anymore. The goblins were searching for someone, or something. Lonjiku Kaijitsu is missing. The seven-pointed star marks everything. And somewhere in the Lost Coast brush, a goblin leader is reporting back to whoever gave him names and a drug-fueled army.

"Do you want us to find him?" you ask.

Ameiko meets your eyes. There's steel there under the fear. "I want to know what he's done. And I want him to answer for it."

The trail leads south. Into darkness. Into questions that need answering before more bodies pile up in Sandpoint's square.

I investigate the Sandpoint Glassworks, where something has clearly gone wrong.

Turn 7

The Glassworks sprawls at Sandpoint's southern edge, built around a furnace hot enough to melt sand into art. Tonight it's a tomb.

The front doors hang open. Inside, the heat is wrong, the furnace should be banked at evening, but it roars like something alive. The air tastes like copper and char. Your eyes water.

Bodies. Three of them. Workers, by their burned hands and the remnants of work clothes. They're arranged, not scattered like they fell, but positioned with deliberate care around the furnace's mouth. Their chests are carved with symbols. Seven-pointed stars, crude but purposeful, cut deep while they still breathed.

Victor swears quietly. Peet kneels beside the nearest corpse, his fingers hovering just above the carved flesh. "Hours ago. While the festival burned," he says flatly. "They were killed slowly. The pain..." He trails off, but you catch it, the echo of agony written in bone and tissue.

Deeper in the workshop, you find Lonjiku Kaijitsu.

He's alive. Barely. Bound to a chair in his private office, blood soaking through a wound in his thigh. His face is swollen, beaten, not by goblins, but by something with more precision. Human hands. He looks up when you enter, and terror floods his eyes.

"Don't, " His voice cracks. "Don't touch anything. They said they'd come back. They said if I moved, if I talked to anyone..." He spits blood. "She said she'd burn the whole town."

She. Not goblins. A woman.

Behind the office desk, you find a ledger. Shipments listed in Lonjiku's careful hand. Payments to the Licktoad tribe. And at the margins, in a different hand, elegant, deliberate, notes in a cipher you recognize. Thassilonian notation. Blood-magic accounting.

Someone is using the Glassworks as a distribution point. Someone is organizing the goblins. Someone knows exactly what she's doing.

And she's coming back.

I confront the person behind the trouble at the Glassworks and force the truth about the goblins and Nualia out of them.

Turn 8

You turn back to Lonjiku, rod held loose at your side. Victor and Peet flank the office doorway, they understand the implication. This man has answers, and you mean to extract them.

"Who is she?" you ask quietly. "The woman who did this."

Lonjiku's face crumples. He's broken already, the beating, the blood loss, the fear have hollowed him out. "Nualia," he whispers. "My daughter. My daughter did this."

The name lands like a stone in still water. Ameiko's half-sister. The aasimar child who was supposed to be dead, killed in a fire years ago. Victor's jaw tightens. Even Peet goes very still.

"She came back three months ago," Lonjiku continues, the words pouring out now that the dam has cracked. "She was... changed. Not the girl I knew. Something had gotten into her, something dark. She had plans. Talked about the old magic, about Thassilon, about a god-thing that needed waking. She said if I helped her, if I let her use the Glassworks to move things, to coordinate..." He swallows hard. "She said she'd let my family live."

"The goblins," you press. "Tonight's raid. What was she looking for?"

"Not what. Who." Lonjiku's eyes roll toward the ceiling. "She's trying to gather them. The bloodmarked. People descended from Thassilonian nobility. She needs them for a ritual, something about harvesting their greed, their ambition, feeding it into a Sihedron anchor. She had a list. Names. Tonight she was checking if any of them were in the festival crowd."

Your blood goes cold. You understand the implications better than most, the notation, the blood-magic, the deliberate targeting of bloodlines. This isn't random cruelty. This is harvesting. Farming souls.

"Where is she now?" Victor asks, his voice tight.

Lonjiku shakes his head. "Thistletop. The old ruin north of here. That's where she's building it, the anchor, the ritual site. But she's not working alone. There's something else there. Something old. And it's hungry."

You need to move. Now. Before she realizes her plan is compromised.

I search the Glassworks for a way down into the tunnels beneath Sandpoint, following the conspiracy underground.

Turn 9

You leave Lonjiku bleeding in his chair and move through the workshop with purpose. Victor checks the furnace room, brick and heat and ash, nothing hidden. Peet drifts through the storage areas, his shade-touched senses attuned to things that don't belong.

It's in the back office that you find it. A trapdoor beneath a rug, iron ring set flush with the floor. Simple. Deliberate. Recently used, the dust pattern around it is fresh.

Victor pulls it open. Darkness below. Stone steps descending at a steep angle, worn smooth by centuries of use. The air that rises from below carries the smell of old water, old stone, and something else. Something that makes your skin prickle. Ancient. Thassilonian.

Peet produces a cold light from between his palms, pale, ghostly, casting everything in shades of grey. The steps go down. Far down. And at the bottom, you can see the beginning of a tunnel that runs east, deeper into the earth beneath Sandpoint.

"This wasn't built for moving glass," Victor says quietly.

You're right. These tunnels predate the Glassworks by centuries. Thassilonian work, almost certainly. Hidden infrastructure from an empire that fell ten thousand years ago. A network running beneath the town. Perfect for moving bodies, moving artifacts, moving ritual components without being seen.

A sound drifts up from below. Footsteps. Deliberate. Approaching.

Someone's down there. Someone who knows these tunnels.

Victor draws one of his short blades. Peet's light flickers, a tell that he's ready. The footsteps grow louder.

Do you descend, or do you set an ambush at the trapdoor?

I press into the catacombs beneath the town, hunting for evidence of Nualia's plan.

Turn 10

You descend. The stone is slick under your boots, worn by footfalls older than memory. Victor takes point, blade ready. Peet's ghostly light casts everything in shades of corpse-pale, and your dark glass rod hums soft against your thigh, a subsonic vibration only you can feel, a weapon waiting.

The tunnel opens into a chamber. Wide. Vaulted. And covered in seven-pointed stars carved into every surface, deep grooves filled with what looks like old blood. Offering niches line the walls. Some hold bone. Some hold rusted armor. Some hold things you don't have names for.

Ritual space. Ancient. Thassilonian, without question. The air tastes like copper and dust and wrongness.

"There," Peet says quietly.

A table sits in the center, covered with papers, a leather journal, and something wrapped in oilcloth. Victor moves to it first, checking the shadows, empty, for now. The papers are a list. Names. Bloodlines. Handwriting in Nualia's hand, neat and precise and filled with annotations. Generations tracked. Lineages marked. Your own name appears halfway down.

*Vyrindra Emberveil, Infernal Half-Blood. Notation-bearer. HIGH PRIORITY.*

Your jaw tightens. She knows what you are. Knows it runs in your blood. That's why the Glassworks murders targeted bloodmarked souls, Thassilonian descendants. People like you. People like Lonjiku's family. She's collecting them for the ritual.

The journal is thicker. Victor flips it open, scanning. Ritual notes. Incantations. References to something called the Sihedron anchor, an artifact that needs feeding. Living sacrifice. Blood-magic. And at the end of every entry, a name: *Lamashtu*. The demon god Nualia serves. The one promising to transform her.

The wrapped bundle sits heavy in Victor's hands. He unwraps it carefully.

A dagger. Seven-pointed star etched along the blade. The metal gleams black and hungry. Sihedron-marked. Ancient. Power radiates from it like heat, and you feel your blood respond, a pull, a hunger, something in your infernal side recognizing greed-magic and wanting to consume it.

"There's more," Peet says.

A passage opens deeper into the earth, lit from within by a sickly green luminescence. The sound of chanting drifts up from below, not words, but something older. Something that hurts to hear.

Footsteps echo from the passage behind you. Multiple. Moving fast. Armed.

Nualia's found you.

I push deeper into the tunnels, dealing with whatever guards Nualia left behind.

Turn 11

You move toward the green light, toward the chanting. Behind you, the footsteps quicken. Victor takes position at your shoulder. Peet raises his hand, and the air around him begins to shimmer.

The passage opens into a vast chamber. And there, arranged in a circle around a runewell that pulses with sickly luminescence, are five figures. Not human. Not quite anything else.

Sinspawn. Thassilonian abominations, hairless, jaw split too wide, their flesh marked with glowing runes that match the ones carved into the stone. They're feeding the runewell with something. Energy. Magic. Their own life-force draining into the artifact at the circle's center, a seven-pointed star carved from black obsidian and thrumming with hunger.

The moment you enter, they turn. No hesitation. No mercy. They know what they are. Disposable. Tools.

The one closest to you screams, a sound that isn't sound, that exists below hearing, and charges.

Victor's first blade comes up fast, finding the creature's shoulder. Blood, black and smoking, spills across the stone. It doesn't slow. It lashes out with claws that shouldn't exist, that tear through reality as much as flesh.

Peet steps sideways into shadow, his form blurring.

The second sinspawn moves to intercept you. Its rune-marked hide pulses brighter as it feeds more power into the runewell. The obsidian star begins to glow red.

You have seconds before whatever Nualia's building here reaches critical mass.

What do you do?

I learn everything I can about Nualia — her ties to Thistletop and what she intends.

Turn 12

You can't learn what isn't here to tell you. The sinspawn don't think. They don't speak. They're meat animated by rune-magic, slaves to the artifact at the circle's center.

But the journal in your pack, Nualia's own words, that's a different matter.

You snap a hand up as the first sinspawn lunges. Counterspell flares from your fingers, and the creature's rune-magic stutters. It staggers, confused, its charge losing momentum.

Victor presses the advantage, his blade finding its throat.

"Keep them off me," you say, pulling the journal free.

Peet's voice drops low, words in a language that predates human kingdoms. Skeletal arms erupt from the stone floor, bone-white and massive, wrapping around the second sinspawn and pinning it prone against the cavern wall. The creature thrashes, shrieking that subsonic scream, but the bones don't yield.

[COMPANION_SPELL_USED:Peet|Shackles of Bone]

You flip through Nualia's journal, scanning. Ritual notes. Incantations. And there, pages near the end, written in her hand, fever-bright:

*Three months since I woke in the temple. Three months since Lamashtu's gift opened my eyes. The old blood calls to the old blood. I can feel them, the Thassilonian descendants scattered through this miserable coastal town. They don't know what they are. What they carry. But I do. The Sihedron anchor requires thirteen souls steeped in greed-magic, fed willingly or otherwise. Once the circle is complete, the gate opens. Karzoug rises. And I become what I was always meant to be, not Nualia Tobyn, the half-burned child. But Her Herald. Lamashtu's instrument on this world.*

*Thistletop will be ready by the new moon. The goblins are gathered. The anchor is prepared. And when I have the bloodmarked souls, the transformation begins.*

*The Sihedron-marked blades are already in the hands of my servants, the lamias who serve the hunger. They will feed the ritual. They will harvest what needs harvesting.*

Lamias. Servants. And Thistletop, that's where she's consolidating power. Where the ritual will complete.

The third sinspawn breaks free of shadow and lunges at you. No time to read more.

Victor moves to intercept, his blades flashing silver in the green light.

I set out for Thistletop to confront the goblin stronghold at the heart of the raids.

Turn 13

You can't leave yet. The sinspawn are still fighting.

Victor's blade catches the third creature across the chest. Black blood sprays. The thing doesn't fall, doesn't even slow, but it gives him an opening. His second blade follows, driving upward toward the jaw.

Peet's bones hold fast around the pinned sinspawn, but the creature's thrashing is tearing at the skeletal structure. The runes on its hide pulse brighter, feeding energy into the runewell at the circle's center. The obsidian star is glowing red now, burning hot enough that you can feel the heat from where you stand.

The first sinspawn, the one you countered, is on its feet again. It turns toward you with single-minded hunger. Its claws rake the air.

You have maybe thirty seconds before that runewell reaches critical threshold and something worse comes through.

Do you fight, or do you run? You can't do both and survive.

I fight my way into Thistletop, cutting through the goblins and the bugbear captain Bruthazmus.

Turn 14

You can't cut through what you haven't reached yet. The sinspawn blocking the passage won't move.

The creature lunges at you, claws extended. You sidestep, letting its momentum carry it past. Your dark glass rod comes up in a smooth arc, subsonic harmonic, tuned to shatter bone and rupture organs. The impact is clean. The sinspawn folds.

Victor presses his advantage. Both blades working now, moving like water around the creature's wild swings. One catches its shoulder. The other finds its ribs. Black blood pools on ancient stone.

The fourth sinspawn breaks free of Peet's grip. Bone splinters. The creature staggers forward, rune-marked hide flickering, and makes for the runewell. It's feeding itself into the artifact deliberately. Sacrificing itself to accelerate the ritual.

Peet raises his hand. Words in the death-tongue pour out. A wave of scarabs erupts from the stone itself, chittering, necrotic, tearing through the creature's flesh in a storm of fangs and wings.

[COMPANION_SPELL_USED:Peet|Wave of Scarabs]

The fifth sinspawn is still feeding the runewell. The obsidian star is burning white-hot now. The chanting from deeper in the earth grows louder. Angrier.

Something is coming through.

The creature you hit with your rod doesn't get back up. The one Victor's working is slowing, bleeding out. But you're running out of time.

"Move!" Victor shouts.

You can fight to the bitter end and see what emerges. Or you can run deeper, toward Nualia, toward the next level of this conspiracy.

What do you do?

I press toward Nualia herself, determined to end her ritual before it can be completed.

Turn 15

You bolt.

The tunnel ahead slopes downward, deeper into darkness. Behind you, the runewell screams. Victor is already moving, his footfalls sharp and urgent. Peet trails, one hand raised, bones rising behind you like a wall to slow whatever's coming through.

The passage opens into a larger chamber. Torches flicker along the walls, actual torches, not ancient magic. Someone's been here recently. Often.

The air smells like copper and burnt offerings.

You skid to a halt. Ahead, the tunnel splits into three directions. No sign of Nualia. No sign of which way she went.

Behind you, something massive moves through the lower chamber. You hear it before you see it. A sound like a god clearing its throat. The runewell's feeding has woken something that was never meant to wake.

Victor grabs your shoulder. "Which way?"

Peet staggers into the chamber, breathing hard. His fingers are shaking. "That thing," he says quietly. "It's not sinspawn. It's something older."

The three tunnels branch like veins. One slopes deeper still. One angles toward what might be the direction of Thistletop, you can feel it in your bones, the way a bloodhound feels a scent. One leads back up, toward the surface.

The creature behind you moves closer. You don't look back.

You have maybe two minutes before that thing catches up. The journal in your pack weighs heavy. Thistletop waits. Nualia waits.

Which tunnel?

I press toward Festival of Fire: Stop Nualia before she completes her ritual at Thistletop — taking the most direct action to advance it, dealing with whoever or whatever stands in the way.

Turn 16

You take the middle tunnel, the one that pulls toward Thistletop like a lodestone.

The passage widens. Torchlight flickers ahead, real flame now, not rune-glow. Voices. Guttural goblin speech mixed with something else, something that sounds almost human but wrong in the throat, words that slide sideways off your comprehension.

Victor draws both blades without a sound. Peet's hand is already moving, death-words gathering at the back of his throat.

You round a bend and the tunnel opens into a vast natural cavern. Daylight bleeds through cracks in the ceiling, you're close to the surface now. Close to Thistletop itself.

And there's an army waiting.

Goblins. Two dozen at least, clustered around a massive pit that drops away into shadow. They're chanting, voices rising and falling in rhythm. At the pit's edge stands a figure that makes your breath catch.

Lamia. Serpent-bodied, scaled, beautiful in the way a poisoned blade is beautiful. Her upper body is human enough, bronze skin, dark eyes that promise cruelty, but from the waist down she's coils of muscle and venom. A Sihedron-marked blade hangs at her side, runes crawling across the steel like living things.

She turns as you enter. Smiles.

"The bloodmarked sorcerer," she says. Her voice is honey poured over broken glass. "Nualia said you'd come. She was hoping you would."

The goblins stop chanting. Every head turns toward you.

Victor whispers, "That's not a fair fight."

"They never are," you reply.

The lamia draws her blade. The runes flare crimson.

"Kill them," she orders. "But bring me the sorcerer alive. The ritual needs her heart still beating."

The goblins shriek and charge.

What do you do?

I press toward Festival of Fire: Stop Nualia before she completes her ritual at Thistletop — taking the most direct action to advance it, dealing with whoever or whatever stands in the way.

Turn 17

You're already in it. The fight is here. You can't skip past twenty-four screaming goblins and a lamia matriarch by wishing harder.

The first wave hits like a storm. Crude spears and rusty blades. Victor moves to meet them, his short blades finding throats with surgical precision. A goblin falls without even understanding what killed it.

Peet steps back, hands rising. Words pour out in the death-tongue, ancient, vicious, carrying weight that makes the air itself recoil. Skeletal hands erupt from the stone floor, massive and brutal, and close around three goblins at once. They scream as bone crushes bone.

[COMPANION_SPELL_USED:Peet|Shackles of Bone]

The lamia doesn't move. Doesn't need to. She watches you with those dark, patient eyes while her army dies around her.

You raise your rod. The subsonic frequencies hum beneath hearing threshold, but the goblins feel it. Some of them drop, clutching their heads. Others press forward anyway, too stupid or too terrified to stop.

A spear grazes your ribs. Fire and blood. You don't think about it. You move.

Your rod connects with a goblin's skull. It crumples. Another lunges from your left, you pivot, let its momentum carry it past, and the rod finds its spine. Subsonic impact. Vertebrae shatter.

Victor is grinning. That feral, terrible grin he gets when the killing is clean. He's dropped four goblins now, moving between them like he's dancing.

But there are still twenty left. And the lamia hasn't moved.

She draws her blade fully now. The Sihedron runes pulse like a heartbeat.

"You're fast," she says to you. "Nualia was right. You'll do nicely."

She moves. Not like a woman. Like a snake, fluid, impossibly quick. Her blade comes at you in an arc that should be blocked but somehow isn't.

The Sihedron blade bites deep into your shoulder. Not your rod-arm. She's skilled. Deliberate. The cut burns, not from the wound itself but from the runes, greed-magic crawling through your blood like poison.

Your vision blurs for just a moment. Long enough for you to feel it: that hunger, that need to take and keep and never let go. The mark recognizing kindred magic in your own bloodline. It whispers.

Victor moves to intercept the lamia, but she's already sliding sideways, coils flowing like liquid. A goblin dies in her wake, crushed between her tail and the stone wall, simply in the wrong place.

The goblins keep coming. Wave after wave of them.

What do you do?

I press toward Festival of Fire: Stop Nualia before she completes her ritual at Thistletop — taking the most direct action to advance it, dealing with whoever or whatever stands in the way.

Turn 18

You stop repeating yourself and start fighting.

The lamia is faster than you. Stronger too. But she's patient, she wants you alive, which means she's holding back. That's the only reason you're still breathing.

You don't waste the advantage.

Your dark glass rod comes up in a vicious arc. The subsonic frequencies build, gathering force, and you drive it straight at her midsection. She twists, scales gleaming, and the impact glances off her ribs instead of punching through. Still enough to stagger her.

Black blood drips from where the rod connected. She hisses, a sound that makes your teeth ache, and comes at you again. Her blade slides past your guard, and you feel the heat of the runes as it passes close enough to singe.

You think about the journal you found. Nualia's entries, growing steadily less human. The handwriting degrading into something between writing and runes.

"She chose this," you say. "That's what the journal showed. She walked into the runewell on purpose."

Victor doesn't look surprised. "Power always picks volunteers. Easier than slaves."

The passage opens into a larger space. You can see light ahead, green, sickly, pulsing. The runewell. And beyond it, deeper down, something else. A chamber. Space that shouldn't exist given Thistletop's geography.

"Thirteen bloodmarked souls," Peet says. "That's what opens the gate. Nualia's been harvesting them through the goblin raids. Through the murders."

You remember the carved bodies in the Glassworks. Seven-pointed stars carved into flesh. Blood-notation. Greed-magic.

"She marked me," you say quietly. It's not a question. You can feel the greed-mark in your arm like a second heartbeat. Like something recognizing something. "I was on that list."

"Because you're half-infernal," Victor says. It's matter-of-fact. No judgment in it. "Karzoug needs bloodmarked souls. You'd be worth more than a dozen ordinary people."

The light from the runewell is getting brighter as you approach. It hurts to look at directly. Not pain exactly. More like your eyes are remembering something they were never meant to see.

"The ritual can't be stopped," Peet says. "Not once it's that far along. I've read enough grimoires to know. You can disrupt it. Delay it. But the fundamental working is already in motion."

"How long?" you ask.

"Until moonrise," Peet says. "The new moon rises tonight. That's the temporal anchor. That's when the gate opens. The ritual doesn't cause it. The ritual just... facilitates. Clears the way."

Victor swears quietly. "So even if we stop Nualia, "

"The gate opens anyway," you finish.

The three of you stand at the edge of the runewell chamber. The green light pulses. Below it, you can feel something vast and patient stirring in its sleep. Ten thousand years of waiting. Ten thousand years of hunger.

"Then we stop her before she finishes the sacrifice," Victor says. "We get those thirteen souls free, we get them out. At least we slow it down."

"Two are already taken," Peet says. "I could sense them as we descended. Two of the thirteen are in the ritual space right now."

You think about that. Two bodies already claimed. Eleven more to go.

"We move," you say. "Now. Before Lucrecia figures out we're not coming back."

Victor and Peet move without argument.

Turn 29

You slow. Raise a hand. Victor stops immediately, years of working together mean he reads the shift in your posture before you speak.

"Traps," you say. "We've been moving too fast."

Peet nods. He understands. Ancient places like this don't stay empty by accident. They stay empty because they kill the curious.

You move forward careful now. Each step deliberate. Your eyes track the stonework, the carved passages, the way the light from the runewell casts long shadows across the walls.

There. A groove in the floor. Shallow but deliberate. Thassilonian work, you can read the rune-carving even from distance. Your infernal heritage lets you sense the magic sleeping in it. Old magic. Patient magic.

You kneel. Trace the groove with one finger without touching it. The groove runs the width of the passage. When pressure applies, weight, footsteps, it triggers something. Probably a collapse. Maybe worse.

"Pressure plate," you tell Victor and Peet. "Whole section of ceiling is rigged."

Victor crouches beside you. He's good at this, reading traps the way most people read faces. "What's the offset?"

You point to the western wall. A section of stone that's slightly raised compared to its neighbours. "We stick to the wall. Step over the groove. Move fast but controlled. No running."

You demonstrate. One hand on the stone for balance. Step over the carved line with precision. The groove doesn't trigger. The ceiling doesn't fall.

Victor follows. Peet comes last, moving with the careful grace of someone who's seen enough magic go wrong to respect its teeth.

The passage opens into a larger chamber. You raise your hand again.

This one is worse.

The floor is a maze of carved lines. Seven-pointed stars arranged in intricate patterns. Your blood-notation education screams warnings. Those aren't just carvings. They're bindings. Magical wards layered thick as paint. Touch the wrong star and something gets called down.

"Glyph trap," you say quietly. "Old one. Probably calls something from the runewell if triggered."

You study the pattern. There. A path through the center. The stars are arranged deliberately, and there's a thread of unpainted stone running between them. Safe passage, if you follow it exactly.

"Single file," you say. "Follow my footsteps. Exactly."

You move into the maze. One step after another. The carved stars pulse with faint light as you pass them, reacting to your presence but not your touch. Your infernal blood probably helps, you're not entirely foreign to Thassilonian magic. It recognizes you as something other than human prey.

The path is narrow. Forces you to move slowly. Behind you, Victor and Peet follow with the same precision. No one breathes hard. No one rushes.

Twenty feet. Thirty. The far edge of the chamber draws closer.

You're almost clear when the passage behind you echoes with sound. Footsteps. Many of them. Goblins, probably. Or worse. Something moving through the lower chambers with purpose.

You don't look back. Just step faster, keeping the path exact. The stars beneath your feet pulse brighter.

Victor curses quietly.

You reach the far wall. The maze falls away behind you. The pursuing sound is getting closer.

The passage ahead slopes downward again. Toward the ritual chamber. Toward Nualia.

"Move," you say.

The three of you break into a run.

I return to Sandpoint to warn Sheriff Hemlock of what's coming and resupply before any assault.

Turn 30

You make the call mid-passage. The sound of pursuit is getting louder. The maze behind you is already triggering. The ritual chamber ahead is still hours away by foot, and you're bleeding, tired, and facing an enemy you don't fully understand yet.

Victor doesn't argue. He knows the math. Three of you against Nualia and whatever ancient thing is waking in that runewell.

The climb back to the surface is brutal. The passages blur. You navigate by memory and instinct, taking the fastest routes you learned on the way down. Behind you, goblin shouts echo off stone, but they don't press. They're afraid of the tunnels. Afraid of what lives in them.

You break into daylight at the Glassworks' trapdoor. The basement is empty. Lonjiku is gone, probably fled to wherever terrified men hide when their world collapses. You seal the trapdoor behind you as best you can and move into Sandpoint's streets.

Late afternoon now. The sun is dropping toward the western horizon. The new moon rises tonight.

Sheriff Hemlock is at the Cathedral. You find him organizing a defensive perimeter, posting men at the gates, checking supplies. He looks older than he did five days ago. The festival has aged him.

He sees you covered in blood and dirt and stops what he's doing.

"They're coming," you tell him. Without preamble. Without explanation he won't understand yet. "From Thistletop. From beneath it. The goblin raids were preparation. The murders were ritual work. Something wakes tonight when the moon rises. If it fully wakes, Sandpoint doesn't survive it."

Hemlock's jaw tightens. "How many?"

"Unknown. Goblins for certain. A lamia matriarch. The woman who started all this, Nualia Tobyn. And something older. Something that's been sleeping under Thistletop for ten thousand years."

You watch him process that. To his credit, he doesn't laugh.

"How long do we have?" he asks.

"Until moonrise. A few hours at best."

Hemlock nods once. Hard. "Then we prepare for siege. I'll get every able-bodied person behind the walls. The cathedral is highest ground. Best defensibility." He's already moving, already thinking like a commander. "Supplies?"

"We need weapons. Anything you can spare. Rope. Oil for fires. And information, I need to know every person who was marked by the goblins. Everyone carved with that seven-pointed star."

"There will be more," Peet says quietly. He's been silent until now, reading the magical currents in the air. "Thirteen was the number. We know two are already claimed in the ritual chamber. That leaves eleven still to be taken or already dead somewhere we haven't found them."

Hemlock doesn't ask how Peet knows this. He's learned not to ask questions about things that happen in the dark.

"I'll put men on a search," Hemlock says. "Anyone matching that description, we bring them in, we protect them." He pauses. "The ones already marked, can they be saved?"

"The ones still living can," you say. You're not entirely sure it's true, but Hemlock needs something to believe in. "If we stop the ritual before moonrise."

"Then that's what we do," Hemlock says.

He turns and starts barking orders. Men scatter to follow them. Within minutes, Sandpoint is moving. Not panic. Organized fear. The kind that builds walls and sharpens blades.

Victor touches your arm. "We need to eat. Rest if we can. We're not any use to anyone half-dead on our feet."

He's right. You're running on adrenaline and spite. Your arm throbs where the greed-mark pulses. Your spells are mostly spent.

I study the greed-mark on me and seek a way to lift it before it worsens.

Turn 33

You find a quiet corner of the cathedral's lower library, parchment, candlelight, the smell of old binding glue. Victor keeps watch at the door. Peet sits across from you, his hollow eyes tracking your movements with the kind of understanding that comes from existing between worlds.

You roll up your sleeve.

The mark has spread since Lucrecia touched you. It crawls from wrist to elbow now, a constellation of seven-pointed stars burned into your skin in copper-bright lines. They don't hurt. That's the worst part. They feel warm. Almost comfortable. When you stare at them too long, you catch yourself thinking about things you want. Things you could take. Things that belong to you if you're bold enough to claim them.

You pull your sleeve down.

"Dispel Magic won't touch it," Peet says quietly. He's been thinking about this since the cavern. "I felt it try. The mark's not a spell, it's a signature. A brand. Thassilonian blood-work written into your flesh."

You do have something, though. Your sealed archive folio, the Emberveil records, partially decoded. You've been carrying your own family's greed-notation lineage for months, studying it in fragments between fights. The same seven-pointed symbol. The same hunger underneath.

You open the folio.

The pages are dense, written in Old Bloodtongue mixed with Arcane Script. Your great-grandmother's hand, you think. Or the one before her. The notation margin notes talk about "anchoring the appetite" and "binding the sign before it binds you." One entry, dated in a year you don't recognize, is marked with a red X and a single phrase: "Lost to the hunger. Burned the body. Marked the name do not attempt resurrection."

There's a ritual described two pages later. Not a spell. A counter-notation.

It requires drawing symbols on your skin using copper-suspension ink, the vial you carry for field work. It requires focusing on the specific geometry of your personal greed-mark and overwriting it with a suppression sequence. And it requires holding perfectly still while your own infernal heritage fights against the foreign magic trying to colonize it.

The ink is messy work. You've done it before in emergencies, quick marks drawn in darkness to ward off worse things. This is different. This is precise. This is asking your blood to choose sides in a war that started before you were born.

"How long?" you ask.

"The ritual? Maybe an hour if you don't make mistakes," Peet says. "The recovery after? Unknown. You're trying to fence off magic that's already digging in. The longer it's been there, the deeper the roots."

Victor appears at the library door. "We're running out of time. Moon's coming up in three hours."

You look at the folio. At your marked arm. At the copper-suspension ink vial in your hand.

The mark is comfortable. That's what frightens you most. If you wait much longer, comfort becomes acceptance. Acceptance becomes integration. And then it stops being a curse and starts being part of you.

You pull out the ink and a long needle.

The work is meticulous and painful. You map the seven-pointed stars burning in your skin, tracing them with your eyes, then begin drawing the suppression sequence in copper ink directly over them. The needle is sharp. The ink burns where it touches.

Peet watches, occasionally pointing to an error in your geometry. The hollow-born understands death-work and binding magic, greed-notation follows similar principles, he says. Anchor, contain, deny. The pattern has to be perfect or it doesn't hold.

By the time you finish, your arm is shaking and your vision is blurred. The copper ink has created a secondary pattern layered over the seven-pointed stars, a glyph-lock meant to starve them of magical anchoring. It's not perfect. But it's holding.

Turn 34

You gather Victor and Peet in the cathedral's war room, what used to be a storage cellar and is now Hemlock's makeshift command post. Maps of Thistletop are spread across a wooden table, sketched from Shalelu's scouting runs and your own desperate escape hours ago.

Turn 35

Thistletop rises across the channel like a broken tooth. The rope bridge sways in the cold wind, and you can feel the age in it, frayed cordage, wooden slats that creak under their own weight. The rope bridge creaks in the wind. Forty feet across, maybe less. Exposed the entire way. If the goblins have organized, if they've got archers positioned in those towers, crossing it becomes a killing ground.

Turn 36

You take a breath. Center yourself. The composure settles over you like armour, real, but it costs.

You're twenty feet from the far side now. The fortress is chaos, goblins running, shouting, trying to figure out what's happening. The courtyard lights are flickering. Something deep below is making the ground shake.

The final ten feet of bridge. No more archers. Just the wind and the rope and the cold knowledge that you're about to step directly into a fortified position held by creatures that have nothing left to lose.

You reach the far platform and step off the bridge onto stone.

Immediately, the first wave hits.

Six goblins, maybe seven, emerging from the gatehouse with spears and short swords. They're panicked but committed. Scared things are the most dangerous kind.

Victor moves through them like water through a sieve.

His first blade opens the nearest goblin's chest cavity. The second blade catches the next one under the jaw and rips upward. He's not fighting efficiently, he's fighting brutally, and the goblins break. Three turn and run toward the inner structures. Two are already dead or dying. The last one tries to back away, tripping over its own feet.

"Don't stop," you tell him.

You're already past them, moving toward the main structure. The alarm is still sounding from below. The ground trembles in rhythm with it, pressure-plates, glyph-wards, something in the runewell responding to disturbance.

Shalelu's mercenaries clear the remaining defenders in the courtyard with brutal efficiency. No wasted motion. No mercy. These are veterans. They understand what's happening. The fortress is about to collapse into chaos, and they need to be deeper in before it does.

Peet moves beside you, his death-sense stretched thin. "The binding," he says quietly. "It's breaking. Nualia is pushing through."

The ritual chamber sprawls before you, vast and impossibly deep. The seven-pointed stars carved into stone glow sickly green, brighter than they were hours ago. The thirteen bodies remain arranged in their pattern, eleven empty vessels, two filled. The stranger you saw before is gone. Only Ameiko's corpse remains, positioned at the gate's threshold like an offering.

Your chest tightens. You already knew. Peet sensed it. But knowing and *seeing* are different things.

Nualia stands at the chamber's center, no longer bound.

Her skin has taken on a scaled quality. Her eyes are luminous, greenish-gold, and ancient beyond measure. When she smiles, her teeth are too many.

"You came back," she says. Her voice echoes wrong, like multiple voices speaking in unison. "I wasn't certain you would. But the mark called you. The greed-magic knows its own."

Behind her, the gate pulses. Reality bends around it like heat shimmer. You can feel something on the other side, vast, patient, aware. Waiting.

Victor draws both blades. "How long until, "

"Moonrise," Nualia interrupts. "Moments. The ritual doesn't need completion now. The mechanism is already in motion. The souls are already bound. All that remains is the opening."

Something screams from deeper still.

Not a goblin. Not anything mortal.

The sinspawn erupts from the deeper passages.

A second creature descends toward Peet. He speaks a single word, *Toll the Dead*, and the sinspawn convulses mid-leap. Its body writhes, necrotic energy eating through its flesh from inside out. It hits the ground already dying, but the third one is already moving.

It comes straight at you.

You have seconds to act. The greed-mark in your arm writhes and *burns*. The ritual chamber's light pours into it like it's been waiting for this moment. Your own magic feels distant, muted, as if the Thassilonian power here is drowning everything else out.

Nualia watches with something like pity. "Let it in," she says softly. "Stop fighting what you are. The mark knows the way. It knows *hunger*. Just let, "

The sinspawn is almost on you.

Behind it, the gate opens wider. You catch a glimpse of what waits beyond, something vast, something that remembers when the world screamed its name.

Victor *reaches* for the *blade* *anyway*.

"*Don't* *touch* it," you *say*. Your *hand* *grabs* his *wrist*. "*It's* *Thassilonian*. *It's* *warded*. Your *fingers* will *burn*."

"*Then* what do you *suggest*?" *he* *says*, and there's *no* *fear* in his *voice*, just *hard* *clarity*. "*We* *have* *minutes* before *this* *thing* *comes* *down*."

*Peet* *leans* *hard* *against* the *stone*, *breathing* *shallow*. The *necrotic* *backlash* *cost* him. *Everything* *costs* him *now*.

You *step* *forward* and *look* at the *blade*.

*Curved*. *Wickedly* *sharp*. *Marked* with the *Sihedron*, *seven-pointed* *star*, *ancient* and *hungry*. The *runes* *carved* along its *edge* are *Thassilonian*, *yes*, but they're *not* *warding* the *blade* itself. They're *warding* the *dam*.

*Karzoug's* *last* *insurance*. If the *lamia* *failed*, the *blade* would *finish* the *work*. *Ruin* *the* *valley*. *Drown* the *town*. *Feed* *chaos* into a *region* that *needed* to *stay* *broken* for his *plans* to *work*.

Your *arm* *pulses* with the *greed-mark*.

*You* *can* *feel* the *blade's* *hunger*. It *wants* to *be* *pulled* *free*. It *wants* the *dam* to *break*. It *wants* to *feed* on the *deaths* that *will* *follow*.

You *raise* your *dark* *glass* *rod*.

"*Step* *back*," you *say*.

*Victor* *doesn't* *move*.

"*Step* *back*, or I *hit* *you* *with* the *rod* and *you* *stay* *back*."

He *steps* *back*.

You *move* *close* to the *blade*, *can* *feel* the *heat* *radiating* from the *runes*. The *water* *sprays* *cold* around you. The *fissure* *opens* *wider*. The *dam* *will* *break* in *moments*.

You *think* of the *people* in *Turtleback* *Ferry*. *Loggers*. *Ferrymen*. *Children* hiding in *basements*, *praying* the *water* doesn't *reach* them. *Families* *scattered* by the *ogre* *raids*, *clinging* to *whatever* *shelter* they *could* *find*.

You *think* of *Syndra*, *withering* in *Port* *Nyanzaru*, *waiting* for you to *stop* the *death* *curse*.

You *swing* the *rod*.

The *subsonic* *frequency* *peaks*. The *dark* *glass* *connects* *with* the *Sihedron-marked* *blade*, and the *impact*

I press toward Defend the town of Turtleback Ferry from ogre raids and prevent the Kreeg ogres and the lamia matriarch Lucrecia from triggering Skull's Crossing dam's collapse. Success saves the valley from catastrophic flooding and hundreds of lives; failure allows the dam to burst, killing hundreds and permanently darkening the campaign's tone. — taking the most direct action to advance it, dealing with whoever or whatever stands in the way.

Turn 148

The subsonic frequency peaks. The dark glass connects with the Sihedron-marked blade, and the impact shatters silence itself.

The runes *scream*.

Not sound. Something worse. A vibration that travels through stone and bone and blood all at once. The blade *rings* like a tuning fork struck by a hammer. The fissure spreads, but wrong. Instead of widening, it *seals*. The cracks knit themselves back together. The water *stops* spraying.

Then silence. Complete, suffocating silence.

You hold the rod steady. Your burned hand trembles. The blade is still there, still wedged, still *hungry*, but the magic animating it has fractured. The runes no longer glow. They're just carved lines now. Thassilonian script with no power behind it.

The dam holds.

Victor exhales. Peet lowers himself to sitting, his dark robes soaked with meltwater. The valley beneath you is still there. The people in Turtleback Ferry are still alive. They don't know it yet. In a few hours, they'll realize the water isn't rising. They'll understand they weren't drowned.

You step back from the blade. Your burned hand screams. You ignore it.

"Lucrecia's dead," Victor says. It's not a question.

"Lucrecia's dead," you confirm.

"The ogres?"

"Scattered. Leaderless. Kaven said the ones that survived the fight are heading back toward Hook Mountain." You turn away from the blade. "It's over. The dam is sealed. The valley is safe."

The weight of it hits you then. Not relief. Something heavier. You've done this before, stopped something ancient and hungry from consuming the world. Sealed gates. Destroyed runewells. Killed things that shouldn't have been killable.

And it's never over.

There's always another blade. Another dam waiting to break. Another piece of Karzoug's machinery still ticking somewhere in the dark.

But not today. Today, the valley lives.

The three of you make your way down from the dam. The path is treacherous, snow, ice, corpses of ogres beginning to freeze in the wind. Kaven meets you halfway down, face grim but alive.

"The garrison at Fort Rannick," he says. "We found survivors in the hills. Seven Black Arrows. They want to march on Hook Mountain and finish the job."

Victor looks at you. Peet is already struggling to walk. The backlash from his necromantic work has left him pale and shaking.

"We could," Victor says carefully. "We could hit them while they're scattered. Make sure they never raid the Lost Coast again."

But Turtleback Ferry is waiting. The people need to see that the threat is gone. They need to bury their dead. They need time to stop being afraid.

Kaven watches you, waiting for the call.

I press toward Infiltrate Jorgenfist, the stone giant fortress, and defeat the stone giant wizard Mokmurian who is raising an army to conquer Varisia — taking the most direct action to advance it, dealing with whoever or whatever stands in the way.

Turn 149

You look at Kaven, then back at the valley below. Smoke still rises from Turtleback Ferry. People are emerging into the snow, staring upriver, waiting for the water that won't come.

"The Black Arrows stay here," you say. "They guard the valley. They make sure the remaining ogres don't regroup. You coordinate with them, Kaven. That's the work that matters now."

Kaven's jaw tightens. He wants vengeance. He wants to hunt the giants back to their mountain and burn what's left. You understand the impulse. You've felt it plenty.

"Mokmurian is already dead," you tell him. "We killed him. The stone giants serving him are scattered or free. Hunting stragglers won't change anything. Holding this valley will."

He nods. Doesn't like it, but he nods.

---

The ride back to Sandpoint takes a full day. Victor's wounds need proper tending. Peet needs rest that goes deeper than sleep, needs time for his burned channels to stop screaming. You need to think.

You've been moving since Xin-Shalast. Three days down from the spire. The ritual chamber. Thistletop. Now the dam. Each step has taken something. Each victory has cost.

Sheriff Hemlock meets you at the garrison gates. He looks like he's aged ten years in the months you've been gone.

"The dam's sealed?" he asks.

"The dam's sealed. Lucrecia's dead. The immediate threat is contained."

Relief flickers across his face. Then it dies. He knows what you mean by *immediate*.

"There's a problem," he says. "Magnimar's been sending ravens. The murders started there three weeks ago. Seven dead, all marked the same way, Sihedron carved into their chests. The city guard is panicking. The Lord-Mayor is demanding answers."

Ameiko steps out from behind him. Her bandages are still fresh, but her eyes are sharp.

"It's Ironbriar," she says. "Justice Court magistrate. He's been running a cult in the city sewers. People are disappearing. There's something down there, something organized. The city watch won't touch it, they think Ironbriar has half the garrison in his pocket."

You remember Ironbriar. Saw him once in passing during the trials after the Skinsaw murders. Pale eyes. Cold smile. A man who knew things he shouldn't.

Victor catches your eye. He's reading the same thing you are.

"Magnimar's a three-day ride," Peet says quietly. He's steadying himself against the wall. "We need rest. We need supplies. And we need to know what we're walking into."

But you already know what you're walking into. Another piece of Karzoug's apparatus. Another cultist feeding greed-marked souls into a ritual that shouldn't exist anymore. The Runelord is dead. The gate is sealed. But his servants are still alive, still moving, still *hungry*.

The fire in the garrison hearth crackles. Outside, snow falls hard. Sandpoint is safe. The valley is safe. But the work isn't finished. It will never be finished.

You think of Syndra Martel, withering in a distant port city. You think of the people whose names you'll never know, scattered across Varisia, who'll die if you don't move fast enough.

"Rest tonight," you say. "We ride for Magnimar at dawn."

Victor nods. Peet looks like he might collapse, but he nods too.

Ameiko steps forward. "I'm coming with you."

"No," you say.

"Yes," she counters. Her voice is steady. "Ironbriar murdered my family. He orchestrated the conspiracy that burned my inn. I know the city. I know the players. And I'm not sitting in Sandpoint while you

I press toward Infiltrate Jorgenfist, the stone giant fortress, and defeat the stone giant wizard Mokmurian who is raising an army to conquer Varisia — taking the most direct action to advance it, dealing with whoever or whatever stands in the way.

Turn 150

You stop her with a look.

"Mokmurian is already dead," you say flatly. "We killed him weeks ago. Jorgenfist is cleared. The arc is finished."

Ameiko's face hardens. "Then why are we still talking about stone giants?"

"We're not," you say. "We're moving forward."

She doesn't like it. But she accepts it.

---

The problem is that you've already *been* to Jorgenfist. Already breached the fortress. Already shattered Mokmurian's bindings and left him conscious for the first time in centuries, fully aware of what Karzoug had made him, before his shattered form gave out and he simply... stopped.

The library beneath that fortress yielded everything you needed: maps to Xin-Shalast. Confirmation of the Runeforge's location. The knowledge that runeforged weapons were the only steel capable of harming Karzoug himself.

You used that knowledge. You went to Xin-Shalast. You killed the Runelord.

The arc closed.

Now there are other threads pulling. Magnimar bleeds. Ironbriar's cult spreads Sihedron marks like a plague. Syndra withers in Port Nyanzaru, and the death curse's clock keeps ticking. The Runeforge still stands, a weapon laboratory sealed beneath a mountain, waiting to forge steel that can cut through magic itself.

But that's the next arc. That's *Sins of the Saviors*, the work of reaching the Runeforge and binding rune-magic into weapons that will matter when Karzoug inevitably rises again, more cautious next time, more careful about his anchors.

Right now, Magnimar is burning.

Ameiko is still standing in front of you, arms crossed, waiting for the real answer.

"Ironbriar," you say. "The murders. The cult in the sewers. That's where we go next."

She nods slowly. She understands. The work shifts, but it doesn't stop.

Victor is already checking his blades. Peet is still pale, but he's moving. The three of you have killed runelords and sealed ancient gates. A magistrate and his pet killers in a city's underbelly is just the next problem wearing a different shape.

"When do we leave?" Ameiko asks.

"Dawn," you say. "Get what you need. Say goodbye to what matters. Magnimar doesn't forgive hesitation."

She turns and walks out. You watch her go, then turn back to the fire.

Victor settles beside you. "Ironbriar won't break easily."

"No," you agree. "But he'll break."

The snow falls harder outside. In three days, you'll be in Magnimar's Justice Court, navigating corruption and lies and the kind of evil that hides behind office doors and judicial seals. You'll find evidence. You'll expose the rot. You'll kill cultists in the dark and drag their conspiracy into light.

But tonight, the fire is warm. Victor is alive. Peet still draws breath.

That's enough for now.
